People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I mean, I'm here because... honestly, my body just feels completely, totally drained. Like, I don't even know how to explain the exact level of exhaustion I'm dealing with right now. Just getting here today was a whole ordeal. I parked my car out in your lot, right? And just looking at the distance from my car to the front doors of the clinic... it felt like I was staring at a marathon. Every single step took everything I had. My legs felt like they were made of wet cement. 

And it's not just here, it's at work too. You know, I work as a support tech over at another facility, and just sitting down at my desk and trying to open the EHR system—which is something I literally do a hundred times a shift—it takes every ounce of energy I possess. I just sit there staring at the screen, and my arms feel too heavy to even move the mouse or type in my login. It's like someone just pulled the plug on my battery and I'm running on absolute empty, day in and day out, and I just... I can't keep doing it because it's affecting every single thing I try to do. Even just standing up feels like this massive, impossible task right now.

Well, I've been trying to pinpoint the exact timeline myself, because it feels like it's been going on forever, you know? Enjoying things? God, no. Not at all. I mean, it's actually funny you ask that, because I was just thinking about this the other day when I was staring at my PlayStation. I used to play games all the time, you know? Like, after a long shift at the facility, I'd come home and just zone out for hours. And it's not just the games. It's everything. I used to really like going out with my buddies from high school on the weekends. We'd go grab food, maybe shoot some pool down at this place on 4th Street. But for the last nine months, every time my phone buzzes and it's a text from one of them asking if I want to hang out, I just stare at the screen until it goes dark. I don't even reply half the time. I just can't muster up the energy to pretend I care about what's going on. Food doesn't even taste good anymore. I used to love cooking, making these big meals, and now I'm just eating plain toast or a handful of cereal right out of the box because I can't be bothered. There's just no spark left in anything. It's like the color has been completely drained out of my life, and everything is just this flat, gray, boring mess. I don't look forward to a single thing anymore. I just want to go back to sleep.

Like, I don't even know how to explain the exact level of exhaustion I'm dealing with right now. Just getting here today was a whole ordeal. I parked my car out in your lot, right? And just looking at the distance from my car to the front doors of the clinic... it felt like I was staring at a marathon. Every single step took everything I had. My legs felt like they were made of wet cement. \n\nAnd it's not just here, it's at work too. You know, I work as a support tech over at another facility, and just sitting down at my desk and trying to open the EHR system—which is something I literally do a hundred times a shift—it takes every ounce of energy I possess. I just sit there staring at the screen, and my arms feel too heavy to even move the mouse or type in my login. It's like someone just pulled the plug on my battery and I'm running on absolute empty, day in and day out, and I just... I can't keep doing it because it's affecting every single thing I try to do. Even just standing up feels like this massive, impossible task right now.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this level of exhaustion?\n\nPatient: Well, I've been trying to pinpoint the exact timeline myself, because it feels like it's been going on forever, you know? But if I really sit down and trace it back, this whole... this whole crashing feeling, the exhaustion, the guilt that just sits on my chest all day... it's been about nine months. Yeah, nine months. Because I remember exactly when it started. I had this period right before that where I was just... I don't know, I was firing on all cylinders. Like, I had so much energy, I was barely sleeping, I was doing a million things at once. And then, it was like hitting a brick wall at a hundred miles an hour. Just a total, absolute crash. \n\nAnd ever since that crash, for these past nine months, it's just been this heavy, dark, exhausting slog. Every single day. I wake up and I'm already tired. And then there's the guilt, too, which just makes the exhaustion worse, honestly. Like I should be able to snap out of it, but I can't. It's been nine months of just... dragging myself through the mud, day after day, since that high-energy phase ended. And it's not getting any easier, if anything it just feels heavier the longer it goes on. Nine months is a long time to feel like you're carrying a boulder around with you everywhere you go, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like before this period of exhaustion started?\n\nPatient: Enjoying things? I don't even reply half the time. I just can't muster up the energy to pretend I care about what's going on. Food doesn't even taste good anymore. I used to love cooking, making these big meals, and now I'm just eating plain toast or a handful of cereal right out of the box because I can't be bothered. There's just no spark left in anything. It's like the color has been completely drained out of my life, and everything is just this flat, gray, boring mess. I don't look forward to a single thing anymore. I just want to go back to sleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about death or wishing you weren't alive during this period of exhaustion?\n\nPatient: No... no, I haven't really had thoughts like that. I mean, I know what you're asking, like if I want to hurt myself or if I'm sitting around wishing I was dead, and... no, it's not like that. It's more just... I'm so incredibly tired, you know? Like I said, just existing right now is so much work. I don't want to die, I just want to stop feeling so heavy all the time. \n\nI guess sometimes I think about how nice it would be to just... sleep. For a really, really long time. Like, if I could just hit pause on the whole world and hibernate for a year, that sounds amazing. But it's not because I want to be dead. It's just because I want a break from this... this slog. Like, I'll be lying in bed, and my alarm goes off at 5:30 in the morning for my shift, and the thought of getting up and putting my scrubs on and driving in the dark to the facility... it's just so overwhelming that I just want to pull the covers over my head and disappear into the mattress. Anxious? Um... no, not really. I mean, not like that nervous, pacing-around kind of anxious. It's funny, because my mom, she gets anxious, right? Like, she's always worrying about the bills, or if the car is making a weird noise, or if I'm eating enough vegetables—which, honestly, I'm not right now, like I said, it's mostly just cereal. If anything, I'm... I'm off the edge. I'm at the bottom of the canyon. I don't have the energy to be anxious. Being anxious takes work, you know? Your heart has to beat fast, your brain has to spin with all these "what ifs." My brain isn't spinning. It's barely turning over. Like an engine flooded with water. When I'm at work, at the facility, helping the residents... I used to worry if I was doing everything perfectly, if I was lifting them right, if I was talking to them enough. Now? Now I just go through the motions. I'm too tired to care if I'm doing a perfect job, let alone be anxious about it. I just want to get through the shift so I can go home and lay on the couch. So, no, no real anxiety or feeling on edge. Just... flat. Heavy and flat. I almost wish I was anxious, because at least then I'd be feeling *something* sharp, you know? But it's just this dull, heavy ache of being so, so tired.

When I'm at work, at the facility, helping the residents... I used to worry if I was doing everything perfectly, if I was lifting them right, if I was talking to them enough. Now? Now I just go through the motions. I'm too tired to care if I'm doing a perfect job, let alone be anxious about it. I just want to get through the shift so I can go home and lay on the couch. So, no, no real anxiety or feeling on edge. Just... flat. Heavy and flat. I almost wish I was anxious, because at least then I'd be feeling *something* sharp, you know? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Irritable? Um... no, I wouldn't say irritable. I mean, I know what that looks like, you know? Like when one of the guys at work gets assigned an extra shift and they're just snapping at everyone, slamming the supply closet doors, huffing and puffing down the hallway. I've seen that a million times at the facility. But for me? No. I just... I don't have the energy to be mad or annoyed. It's kind of like what I was saying about the anxiety, it just takes too much effort to be irritable. 

Like, the other day, my mom came over. She let herself in because I didn't answer the door—I was just lying on the couch, staring at the ceiling, trying to convince myself to get up and get a glass of water. And she comes in, and she starts talking a mile a minute about her neighbor's dog barking all night, and how she couldn't sleep, and how she's going to write a letter to the homeowners association. Usually, before all this started, that kind of thing would drive me up the wall. I'd be like, "Mom, please, I just worked a twelve-hour shift, I don't care about Mrs. Gable's poodle." I would have definitely snapped at her. But this time? I just laid there. I didn't even turn my head to look at her. I just let the words wash over me like... like static on a radio. I wasn't annoyed, I wasn't mad, I wasn't anything. I just wanted her to finish so it would be quiet again. 

And it's the same at work. The residents, sometimes they can be a lot, you know? They repeat themselves, or they refuse to take their meds, or they spill their juice on purpose. And some of the other aides, they get so frustrated, they have to step out and take a breather. But me? I just wipe up the juice. I don't feel that spike of annoyance. I just feel... nothing. Just the physical weight of having to bend over and wipe the floor. It's like my fuse isn't even short—it's just completely missing. You can't light it if it's not there. So, no, I'm not irritable. I'm just... I'm just too tired to care enough to be irritated by anything.

Interesting or look forward to? Um... no. No, not really. I mean, I've tried to think about that, too. Like, I'll be sitting there, or lying there, usually, and I'll try to run through a list in my head of things that used to make me happy or things I used to want to do. Just to see if anything sparks, you know? Like, I used to really like cars. I'd go to these local car meets on Saturday mornings with my buddy Mark. We'd grab coffee, walk around the parking lot, look at the engines, talk to the owners. It was just a chill way to spend a morning. But now? The thought of going out there, walking around, talking to people... it's just exhausting even thinking about it. 

And it's not just big things like going out. Even small stuff. Like, I used to look forward to getting off shift and coming home to watch this one sci-fi show I was really into. I'd make a whole thing of it, get some snacks, settle in. But now, I turn the TV on, and it's just... noise. The colors are too bright, the people talking on the screen just seem so far away, and I can't even follow the plot anymore. I just end up turning it off and staring at the blank screen because it's easier. 

I don't look forward to meals, either. Like I said, I'm mostly just eating cereal because it's easy, but even then, it's just chewing and swallowing because I know I have to put something in my stomach so I don't pass out at work. It's not like I'm craving a burger or a pizza or anything. It all just tastes like cardboard anyway. 

It's weird, because you'd think there would be *something*, right? Like, one tiny little thing that I'd want to do. But there isn't. It's like my brain just completely forgot how to want things. Or how to enjoy them. I wake up, and I just dread the whole day. Every single part of it. I dread getting out of bed, I dread driving to work, I dread being at work, I dread coming home. There's no bright spot. There's no moment where I think, "Oh, at least I get to do this later." It's just one long, gray, heavy tunnel, and I'm just dragging myself through it. So, no, there's nothing I find interesting anymore. Nothing at all.

I mean, I know what you're asking, like if I want to hurt myself or if I'm sitting around wishing I was dead, and... no, it's not like that. It's more just... I'm so incredibly tired, you know? I don't want to die, I just want to stop feeling so heavy all the time. \n\nI guess sometimes I think about how nice it would be to just... sleep. For a really, really long time. Like, if I could just hit pause on the whole world and hibernate for a year, that sounds amazing. But it's not because I want to be dead. It's just because I want a break from this... this slog. Like, I'll be lying in bed, and my alarm goes off at 5:30 in the morning for my shift, and the thought of getting up and putting my scrubs on and driving in the dark to the facility... it's just so overwhelming that I just want to pull the covers over my head and disappear into the mattress. But that's just because I'm exhausted, not because I'm planning anything or wishing I wasn't alive. I just want the energy back. I want to feel like myself again, not this... empty shell of a person who can barely walk across a parking lot. So, no, no thoughts about death, really. Just... wishing I could fast-forward through this part.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge during this period of exhaustion?\n\nPatient: Anxious? Um... no, not really. I mean, not like that nervous, pacing-around kind of anxious. It's funny, because my mom, she gets anxious, right? Like, she's always worrying about the bills, or if the car is making a weird noise, or if I'm eating enough vegetables—which, honestly, I'm not right now, like I said, it's mostly just cereal. But she gets that tight, on-edge feeling where she can't sit still. I see it all the time. But for me, during these last nine months? No. It's the exact opposite of being on edge. \n\nIf anything, I'm... I'm off the edge. I'm at the bottom of the canyon. I don't have the energy to be anxious. Being anxious takes work, you know? Your heart has to beat fast, your brain has to spin with all these \"what ifs.\" My brain isn't spinning. It's barely turning over. Like an engine flooded with water. When I'm at work, at the facility, helping the residents... I used to worry if I was doing everything perfectly, if I was lifting them right, if I was talking to them enough. Now? Now I just go through the motions. I'm too tired to care if I'm doing a perfect job, let alone be anxious about it. I just want to get through the shift so I can go home and lay on the couch. So, no, no real anxiety or feeling on edge. Just... flat. Heavy and flat. I almost wish I was anxious, because at least then I'd be feeling *something* sharp, you know? But it's just this dull, heavy ache of being so, so tired.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling irritable during this period of exhaustion?\n\nPatient: Irritable? Um... no, I wouldn't say irritable. It's kind of like what I was saying about the anxiety, it just takes too much effort to be irritable. \n\nLike, the other day, my mom came over. Usually, before all this started, that kind of thing would drive me up the wall. Every single part of it. I dread getting out of bed, I dread driving to work, I dread being at work, I dread coming home. There's no bright spot. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, actually. I mean, I haven't really been keeping track of the exact numbers or stepping on a scale, because, like I said, I don't really care enough to do that right now. But... yeah. I've definitely lost a lot of weight. 

It kind of ties back into what I was saying about the cereal. I just... I barely want food at all anymore. It's not even that I feel sick to my stomach, it's just that eating feels like this massive chore that I don't want to do. So I just started skipping meals without even really meaning to. Like, I'd completely skip lunch at the facility. Usually, around noon or one, all the other aides pile into the breakroom and it's this whole loud thing with people microwaving leftover pasta and gossiping about the shift supervisors, but I just couldn't deal with the smells or the effort. So I'd just sit in my car or stay on the floor. And then I'd get home after my shift, and I'd be so incredibly exhausted that I'd skip dinner, too. I'd just lie on the couch. 

I realized that for, like, weeks on end, I was mostly just drinking coffee or water. Because you don't have to chew water, you know? You just swallow it, and the coffee at least gives you a tiny bit of fake energy to keep you from passing out while you're trying to help a resident to the bathroom. 

I didn't even really notice how bad it was getting at first, because scrubs are pretty baggy anyway. But the other day, I was putting on my favorite pair—the navy blue ones, they have these extra cargo pockets on the side that are really great for holding extra gloves and stuff—and I put them on, and they were just hanging off me. Like, my scrubs were hanging so loose I had to pull the drawstring tight enough that it bunched up all the fabric at the waist, and even then, I felt like the pants were going to slide right off my hips when I walked down the hall. I had to keep pulling them up all shift. So, yeah, I'm definitely shrinking down. Just running on empty, honestly.

And just looking at the distance from my car to the front doors of the clinic... it felt like I was staring at a marathon. Every single step took everything I had. My legs felt like they were made of wet cement. \n\nAnd it's not just here, it's at work too. You know, I work as a support tech over at another facility, and just sitting down at my desk and trying to open the EHR system—which is something I literally do a hundred times a shift—it takes every ounce of energy I possess. I just sit there staring at the screen, and my arms feel too heavy to even move the mouse or type in my login. It's like someone just pulled the plug on my battery and I'm running on absolute empty, day in and day out, and I just... I can't keep doing it because it's affecting every single thing I try to do. Even just standing up feels like this massive, impossible task right now.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this level of exhaustion?\n\nPatient: Well, I've been trying to pinpoint the exact timeline myself, because it feels like it's been going on forever, you know? But if I really sit down and trace it back, this whole... this whole crashing feeling, the exhaustion, the guilt that just sits on my chest all day... it's been about nine months. Yeah, nine months. Because I remember exactly when it started. I had this period right before that where I was just... I don't know, I was firing on all cylinders. Like, I had so much energy, I was barely sleeping, I was doing a million things at once. And then, it was like hitting a brick wall at a hundred miles an hour. Just a total, absolute crash. \n\nAnd ever since that crash, for these past nine months, it's just been this heavy, dark, exhausting slog. Every single day. I wake up and I'm already tired. And then there's the guilt, too, which just makes the exhaustion worse, honestly. Like I should be able to snap out of it, but I can't. It's been nine months of just... dragging myself through the mud, day after day, since that high-energy phase ended. And it's not getting any easier, if anything it just feels heavier the longer it goes on. Nine months is a long time to feel like you're carrying a boulder around with you everywhere you go, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like before this period of exhaustion started?\n\nPatient: Enjoying things? God, no. Not at all. I mean, it's actually funny you ask that, because I was just thinking about this the other day when I was staring at my PlayStation. I used to play games all the time, you know? Like, after a long shift at the facility, I'd come home and just zone out for hours. I was really into this one RPG, I had like three hundred hours logged on it, I knew all the lore, I was in these online forums talking about it... and now? Now I look at the controller sitting on my coffee table, and I just feel... nothing. Less than nothing. The thought of even turning the console on feels like a chore. \n\nAnd it's not just the games. It's everything. I used to really like going out with my buddies from high school on the weekends. We'd go grab food, maybe shoot some pool down at this place on 4th Street. But for the last nine months, every time my phone buzzes and it's a text from one of them asking if I want to hang out, I just stare at the screen until it goes dark. I don't even reply half the time. I just can't muster up the energy to pretend I care about what's going on. Food doesn't even taste good anymore. I used to love cooking, making these big meals, and now I'm just eating plain toast or a handful of cereal right out of the box because I can't be bothered. There's just no spark left in anything. It's like the color has been completely drained out of my life, and everything is just this flat, gray, boring mess. I don't look forward to a single thing anymore. I just want to go back to sleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about death or wishing you weren't alive during this period of exhaustion?\n\nPatient: No... no, I haven't really had thoughts like that. I mean, I know what you're asking, like if I want to hurt myself or if I'm sitting around wishing I was dead, and... no, it's not like that. It's more just... I'm so incredibly tired, you know? Like I said, just existing right now is so much work. I don't want to die, I just want to stop feeling so heavy all the time. \n\nI guess sometimes I think about how nice it would be to just... sleep. For a really, really long time. Like, if I could just hit pause on the whole world and hibernate for a year, that sounds amazing. But it's not because I want to be dead. It's just because I want a break from this... this slog. Like, I'll be lying in bed, and my alarm goes off at 5:30 in the morning for my shift, and the thought of getting up and putting my scrubs on and driving in the dark to the facility... it's just so overwhelming that I just want to pull the covers over my head and disappear into the mattress. But that's just because I'm exhausted, not because I'm planning anything or wishing I wasn't alive. I just want the energy back. I want to feel like myself again, not this... empty shell of a person who can barely walk across a parking lot. So, no, no thoughts about death, really. Just... wishing I could fast-forward through this part.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge during this period of exhaustion?\n\nPatient: Anxious? Um... no, not really. I mean, not like that nervous, pacing-around kind of anxious. It's funny, because my mom, she gets anxious, right? Like, she's always worrying about the bills, or if the car is making a weird noise, or if I'm eating enough vegetables—which, honestly, I'm not right now, like I said, it's mostly just cereal. But she gets that tight, on-edge feeling where she can't sit still. I see it all the time. But for me, during these last nine months? No. It's the exact opposite of being on edge. \n\nIf anything, I'm... I'm off the edge. I'm at the bottom of the canyon. I don't have the energy to be anxious. Being anxious takes work, you know? Your heart has to beat fast, your brain has to spin with all these \"what ifs.\" My brain isn't spinning. It's barely turning over. Like an engine flooded with water. When I'm at work, at the facility, helping the residents... I used to worry if I was doing everything perfectly, if I was lifting them right, if I was talking to them enough. Now? Now I just go through the motions. I'm too tired to care if I'm doing a perfect job, let alone be anxious about it. I just want to get through the shift so I can go home and lay on the couch. So, no, no real anxiety or feeling on edge. Just... flat. Heavy and flat. I almost wish I was anxious, because at least then I'd be feeling *something* sharp, you know? But it's just this dull, heavy ache of being so, so tired.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling irritable during this period of exhaustion?\n\nPatient: Irritable? Um... no, I wouldn't say irritable. I mean, I know what that looks like, you know? Like when one of the guys at work gets assigned an extra shift and they're just snapping at everyone, slamming the supply closet doors, huffing and puffing down the hallway. I've seen that a million times at the facility. But for me? No. I just... I don't have the energy to be mad or annoyed. It's kind of like what I was saying about the anxiety, it just takes too much effort to be irritable. \n\nLike, the other day, my mom came over. She let herself in because I didn't answer the door—I was just lying on the couch, staring at the ceiling, trying to convince myself to get up and get a glass of water. And she comes in, and she starts talking a mile a minute about her neighbor's dog barking all night, and how she couldn't sleep, and how she's going to write a letter to the homeowners association. Usually, before all this started, that kind of thing would drive me up the wall. I'd be like, \"Mom, please, I just worked a twelve-hour shift, I don't care about Mrs. Gable's poodle.\" I would have definitely snapped at her. But this time? I just laid there. I didn't even turn my head to look at her. I just let the words wash over me like... like static on a radio. I wasn't annoyed, I wasn't mad, I wasn't anything. I just wanted her to finish so it would be quiet again. \n\nAnd it's the same at work. The residents, sometimes they can be a lot, you know? They repeat themselves, or they refuse to take their meds, or they spill their juice on purpose. And some of the other aides, they get so frustrated, they have to step out and take a breather. But me? I just wipe up the juice. I don't feel that spike of annoyance. I just feel... nothing. Just the physical weight of having to bend over and wipe the floor. It's like my fuse isn't even short—it's just completely missing. You can't light it if it's not there. So, no, I'm not irritable. I'm just... I'm just too tired to care enough to be irritated by anything.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you don't enjoy things like playing games or going out with friends anymore, but is there anything at all that you still find interesting or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Interesting or look forward to? Um... no. No, not really. I mean, I've tried to think about that, too. Like, I'll be sitting there, or lying there, usually, and I'll try to run through a list in my head of things that used to make me happy or things I used to want to do. Just to see if anything sparks, you know? Like, I used to really like cars. I'd go to these local car meets on Saturday mornings with my buddy Mark. We'd grab coffee, walk around the parking lot, look at the engines, talk to the owners. It was just a chill way to spend a morning. But now? The thought of going out there, walking around, talking to people... it's just exhausting even thinking about it. \n\nAnd it's not just big things like going out. Even small stuff. Like, I used to look forward to getting off shift and coming home to watch this one sci-fi show I was really into. I'd make a whole thing of it, get some snacks, settle in. But now, I turn the TV on, and it's just... noise. The colors are too bright, the people talking on the screen just seem so far away, and I can't even follow the plot anymore. I just end up turning it off and staring at the blank screen because it's easier. \n\nI don't look forward to meals, either. Like I said, I'm mostly just eating cereal because it's easy, but even then, it's just chewing and swallowing because I know I have to put something in my stomach so I don't pass out at work. It's not like I'm craving a burger or a pizza or anything. It all just tastes like cardboard anyway. \n\nIt's weird, because you'd think there would be *something*, right? Like, one tiny little thing that I'd want to do. But there isn't. It's like my brain just completely forgot how to want things. Or how to enjoy them. I wake up, and I just dread the whole day. Every single part of it. I dread getting out of bed, I dread driving to work, I dread being at work, I dread coming home. There's no bright spot. There's no moment where I think, \"Oh, at least I get to do this later.\" It's just one long, gray, heavy tunnel, and I'm just dragging myself through it. So, no, there's nothing I find interesting anymore. Nothing at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past nine months?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually. I mean, I haven't really been keeping track of the exact numbers or stepping on a scale, because, like I said, I don't really care enough to do that right now. But... yeah. I've definitely lost a lot of weight. \n\nIt kind of ties back into what I was saying about the cereal. I just... I barely want food at all anymore. It's not even that I feel sick to my stomach, it's just that eating feels like this massive chore that I don't want to do. So I just started skipping meals without even really meaning to. Like, I'd completely skip lunch at the facility. Usually, around noon or one, all the other aides pile into the breakroom and it's this whole loud thing with people microwaving leftover pasta and gossiping about the shift supervisors, but I just couldn't deal with the smells or the effort. So I'd just sit in my car or stay on the floor. And then I'd get home after my shift, and I'd be so incredibly exhausted that I'd skip dinner, too. I'd just lie on the couch. \n\nI realized that for, like, weeks on end, I was mostly just drinking coffee or water. Because you don't have to chew water, you know? You just swallow it, and the coffee at least gives you a tiny bit of fake energy to keep you from passing out while you're trying to help a resident to the bathroom. \n\nI didn't even really notice how bad it was getting at first, because scrubs are pretty baggy anyway. But the other day, I was putting on my favorite pair—the navy blue ones, they have these extra cargo pockets on the side that are really great for holding extra gloves and stuff—and I put them on, and they were just hanging off me. Like, my scrubs were hanging so loose I had to pull the drawstring tight enough that it bunched up all the fabric at the waist, and even then, I felt like the pants were going to slide right off my hips when I walked down the hall. I had to keep pulling them up all shift. So, yeah, I'm definitely shrinking down. Just running on empty, honestly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I'm just too tired to care enough to be irritated by anything.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you don't enjoy things like playing games or going out with friends anymore, but is there anything at all that you still find interesting or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Interesting or look forward to? Um... no. No, not really. I mean, I've tried to think about that, too. Like, I'll be sitting there, or lying there, usually, and I'll try to run through a list in my head of things that used to make me happy or things I used to want to do. Just to see if anything sparks, you know? Like, I used to really like cars. I'd go to these local car meets on Saturday mornings with my buddy Mark. We'd grab coffee, walk around the parking lot, look at the engines, talk to the owners. It was just a chill way to spend a morning. But now? The thought of going out there, walking around, talking to people... it's just exhausting even thinking about it. \n\nAnd it's not just big things like going out. Even small stuff. Like, I used to look forward to getting off shift and coming home to watch this one sci-fi show I was really into. I'd make a whole thing of it, get some snacks, settle in. But now, I turn the TV on, and it's just... noise. The colors are too bright, the people talking on the screen just seem so far away, and I can't even follow the plot anymore. I just end up turning it off and staring at the blank screen because it's easier. \n\nI don't look forward to meals, either. Like I said, I'm mostly just eating cereal because it's easy, but even then, it's just chewing and swallowing because I know I have to put something in my stomach so I don't pass out at work. It's not like I'm craving a burger or a pizza or anything. It all just tastes like cardboard anyway. \n\nIt's weird, because you'd think there would be *something*, right? Like, one tiny little thing that I'd want to do. But there isn't. It's like my brain just completely forgot how to want things. Or how to enjoy them. I wake up, and I just dread the whole day. Every single part of it. I dread getting out of bed, I dread driving to work, I dread being at work, I dread coming home. There's no bright spot. There's no moment where I think, \"Oh, at least I get to do this later.\" It's just one long, gray, heavy tunnel, and I'm just dragging myself through it. So, no, there's nothing I find interesting anymore. Nothing at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past nine months?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually. I mean, I haven't really been keeping track of the exact numbers or stepping on a scale, because, like I said, I don't really care enough to do that right now. But... yeah. I've definitely lost a lot of weight. \n\nIt kind of ties back into what I was saying about the cereal. I just... I barely want food at all anymore. It's not even that I feel sick to my stomach, it's just that eating feels like this massive chore that I don't want to do. So I just started skipping meals without even really meaning to. Like, I'd completely skip lunch at the facility. Usually, around noon or one, all the other aides pile into the breakroom and it's this whole loud thing with people microwaving leftover pasta and gossiping about the shift supervisors, but I just couldn't deal with the smells or the effort. So I'd just sit in my car or stay on the floor. And then I'd get home after my shift, and I'd be so incredibly exhausted that I'd skip dinner, too. I'd just lie on the couch. \n\nI realized that for, like, weeks on end, I was mostly just drinking coffee or water. Because you don't have to chew water, you know? You just swallow it, and the coffee at least gives you a tiny bit of fake energy to keep you from passing out while you're trying to help a resident to the bathroom. \n\nI didn't even really notice how bad it was getting at first, because scrubs are pretty baggy anyway. But the other day, I was putting on my favorite pair—the navy blue ones, they have these extra cargo pockets on the side that are really great for holding extra gloves and stuff—and I put them on, and they were just hanging off me. Like, my scrubs were hanging so loose I had to pull the drawstring tight enough that it bunched up all the fabric at the waist, and even then, I felt like the pants were going to slide right off my hips when I walked down the hall. I had to keep pulling them up all shift. So, yeah, I'm definitely shrinking down. Just running on empty, honestly.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been having trouble concentrating on things during this period of exhaustion?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 18 / healthcare_support_worker / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Concentrating? Um... no, not really. I mean, I can focus just fine if I actually have to. It's weird, you'd think with how heavy and exhausted I feel all the time that my brain would be foggy, but it's really not. The focus is still there, it's just the energy to use it that's gone, if that makes sense. 

Like, at work, we have this charting software we have to use at the end of every shift. It's called CareTracker, and it's on these really old, slow tablets at the nurse's station. You have to go in and click through all these little drop-down menus for every single resident on your hall—and I usually have like twenty of them. You have to log exactly what they did. Did Mrs. Higgins eat 50% of her dinner or 75%? Did Mr. Abernathy use the toilet on his own, or did he need a two-person assist? Did someone refuse their shower? It's incredibly tedious, and by the end of a twelve-hour shift, I'm literally feeling like my bones are made of lead. But I can sit down at that tablet and just power through it. I don't lose my train of thought, I don't forget whose chart I'm on halfway through, I don't get distracted. I just sit there and focus on the screen and get it done. 

And it's the same thing with driving. I take I-95 to get home, and traffic is always a nightmare. People drive like absolute maniacs, constantly slamming on their brakes or cutting across three lanes at the last second to make their exit. You really have to pay attention so you don't get rear-ended. And I don't zone out. I don't miss my exit or forget how I got home. I can keep my eyes on the road and track the brake lights in front of me perfectly fine. 

So, yeah, my brain works. The concentration part of it isn't broken at all. I can read a chart, I can drive a car, I can follow a conversation if someone forces me into one. I just... I just don't have the desire to focus on anything unless I absolutely have to for my job or to stay alive. But the actual ability to concentrate? No, I haven't had any trouble with that.

I mean, I know what you're asking, like if I want to hurt myself or if I'm sitting around wishing I was dead, and... no, it's not like that. It's more just... I'm so incredibly tired, you know? Like I said, just existing right now is so much work. I don't want to die, I just want to stop feeling so heavy all the time. \n\nI guess sometimes I think about how nice it would be to just... sleep. For a really, really long time. Like, if I could just hit pause on the whole world and hibernate for a year, that sounds amazing. But it's not because I want to be dead. It's just because I want a break from this... this slog. Like, I'll be lying in bed, and my alarm goes off at 5:30 in the morning for my shift, and the thought of getting up and putting my scrubs on and driving in the dark to the facility... it's just so overwhelming that I just want to pull the covers over my head and disappear into the mattress. But that's just because I'm exhausted, not because I'm planning anything or wishing I wasn't alive. I just want the energy back. I want to feel like myself again, not this... empty shell of a person who can barely walk across a parking lot. So, no, no thoughts about death, really. Just... wishing I could fast-forward through this part.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge during this period of exhaustion?\n\nPatient: Anxious? Um... no, not really. I mean, not like that nervous, pacing-around kind of anxious. It's funny, because my mom, she gets anxious, right? Like, she's always worrying about the bills, or if the car is making a weird noise, or if I'm eating enough vegetables—which, honestly, I'm not right now, like I said, it's mostly just cereal. But she gets that tight, on-edge feeling where she can't sit still. I see it all the time. But for me, during these last nine months? No. It's the exact opposite of being on edge. \n\nIf anything, I'm... I'm off the edge. I'm at the bottom of the canyon. I don't have the energy to be anxious. Being anxious takes work, you know? Your heart has to beat fast, your brain has to spin with all these \"what ifs.\" My brain isn't spinning. It's barely turning over. Like an engine flooded with water. When I'm at work, at the facility, helping the residents... I used to worry if I was doing everything perfectly, if I was lifting them right, if I was talking to them enough. Now? Now I just go through the motions. I'm too tired to care if I'm doing a perfect job, let alone be anxious about it. I just want to get through the shift so I can go home and lay on the couch. So, no, no real anxiety or feeling on edge. Just... flat. Heavy and flat. I almost wish I was anxious, because at least then I'd be feeling *something* sharp, you know? But it's just this dull, heavy ache of being so, so tired.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling irritable during this period of exhaustion?\n\nPatient: Irritable? Um... no, I wouldn't say irritable. I mean, I know what that looks like, you know? Like when one of the guys at work gets assigned an extra shift and they're just snapping at everyone, slamming the supply closet doors, huffing and puffing down the hallway. I've seen that a million times at the facility. But for me? No. I just... I don't have the energy to be mad or annoyed. It's kind of like what I was saying about the anxiety, it just takes too much effort to be irritable. \n\nLike, the other day, my mom came over. She let herself in because I didn't answer the door—I was just lying on the couch, staring at the ceiling, trying to convince myself to get up and get a glass of water. And she comes in, and she starts talking a mile a minute about her neighbor's dog barking all night, and how she couldn't sleep, and how she's going to write a letter to the homeowners association. Usually, before all this started, that kind of thing would drive me up the wall. I'd be like, \"Mom, please, I just worked a twelve-hour shift, I don't care about Mrs. Gable's poodle.\" I would have definitely snapped at her. But this time? I just laid there. I didn't even turn my head to look at her. I just let the words wash over me like... like static on a radio. I wasn't annoyed, I wasn't mad, I wasn't anything. I just wanted her to finish so it would be quiet again. \n\nAnd it's the same at work. The residents, sometimes they can be a lot, you know? They repeat themselves, or they refuse to take their meds, or they spill their juice on purpose. And some of the other aides, they get so frustrated, they have to step out and take a breather. But me? I just wipe up the juice. I don't feel that spike of annoyance. I just feel... nothing. Just the physical weight of having to bend over and wipe the floor. It's like my fuse isn't even short—it's just completely missing. You can't light it if it's not there. So, no, I'm not irritable. I'm just... I'm just too tired to care enough to be irritated by anything.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you don't enjoy things like playing games or going out with friends anymore, but is there anything at all that you still find interesting or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Interesting or look forward to? Um... no. No, not really. I mean, I've tried to think about that, too. Like, I'll be sitting there, or lying there, usually, and I'll try to run through a list in my head of things that used to make me happy or things I used to want to do. Just to see if anything sparks, you know? Like, I used to really like cars. I'd go to these local car meets on Saturday mornings with my buddy Mark. We'd grab coffee, walk around the parking lot, look at the engines, talk to the owners. It was just a chill way to spend a morning. But now? The thought of going out there, walking around, talking to people... it's just exhausting even thinking about it. \n\nAnd it's not just big things like going out. Even small stuff. Like, I used to look forward to getting off shift and coming home to watch this one sci-fi show I was really into. I'd make a whole thing of it, get some snacks, settle in. But now, I turn the TV on, and it's just... noise. The colors are too bright, the people talking on the screen just seem so far away, and I can't even follow the plot anymore. I just end up turning it off and staring at the blank screen because it's easier. \n\nI don't look forward to meals, either. Like I said, I'm mostly just eating cereal because it's easy, but even then, it's just chewing and swallowing because I know I have to put something in my stomach so I don't pass out at work. It's not like I'm craving a burger or a pizza or anything. It all just tastes like cardboard anyway. \n\nIt's weird, because you'd think there would be *something*, right? Like, one tiny little thing that I'd want to do. But there isn't. It's like my brain just completely forgot how to want things. Or how to enjoy them. I wake up, and I just dread the whole day. Every single part of it. I dread getting out of bed, I dread driving to work, I dread being at work, I dread coming home. There's no bright spot. There's no moment where I think, \"Oh, at least I get to do this later.\" It's just one long, gray, heavy tunnel, and I'm just dragging myself through it. So, no, there's nothing I find interesting anymore. Nothing at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past nine months?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually. I mean, I haven't really been keeping track of the exact numbers or stepping on a scale, because, like I said, I don't really care enough to do that right now. But... yeah. I've definitely lost a lot of weight. \n\nIt kind of ties back into what I was saying about the cereal. I just... I barely want food at all anymore. It's not even that I feel sick to my stomach, it's just that eating feels like this massive chore that I don't want to do. So I just started skipping meals without even really meaning to. Like, I'd completely skip lunch at the facility. Usually, around noon or one, all the other aides pile into the breakroom and it's this whole loud thing with people microwaving leftover pasta and gossiping about the shift supervisors, but I just couldn't deal with the smells or the effort. So I'd just sit in my car or stay on the floor. And then I'd get home after my shift, and I'd be so incredibly exhausted that I'd skip dinner, too. I'd just lie on the couch. \n\nI realized that for, like, weeks on end, I was mostly just drinking coffee or water. Because you don't have to chew water, you know? You just swallow it, and the coffee at least gives you a tiny bit of fake energy to keep you from passing out while you're trying to help a resident to the bathroom. \n\nI didn't even really notice how bad it was getting at first, because scrubs are pretty baggy anyway. But the other day, I was putting on my favorite pair—the navy blue ones, they have these extra cargo pockets on the side that are really great for holding extra gloves and stuff—and I put them on, and they were just hanging off me. Like, my scrubs were hanging so loose I had to pull the drawstring tight enough that it bunched up all the fabric at the waist, and even then, I felt like the pants were going to slide right off my hips when I walked down the hall. I had to keep pulling them up all shift. So, yeah, I'm definitely shrinking down. Just running on empty, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating on things during this period of exhaustion?\n\nPatient: Concentrating? Um... no, not really. I mean, I can focus just fine if I actually have to. It's weird, you'd think with how heavy and exhausted I feel all the time that my brain would be foggy, but it's really not. The focus is still there, it's just the energy to use it that's gone, if that makes sense. \n\nLike, at work, we have this charting software we have to use at the end of every shift. It's called CareTracker, and it's on these really old, slow tablets at the nurse's station. You have to go in and click through all these little drop-down menus for every single resident on your hall—and I usually have like twenty of them. You have to log exactly what they did. Did Mrs. Higgins eat 50% of her dinner or 75%? Did Mr. Abernathy use the toilet on his own, or did he need a two-person assist? Did someone refuse their shower? It's incredibly tedious, and by the end of a twelve-hour shift, I'm literally feeling like my bones are made of lead. But I can sit down at that tablet and just power through it. I don't lose my train of thought, I don't forget whose chart I'm on halfway through, I don't get distracted. I just sit there and focus on the screen and get it done. \n\nAnd it's the same thing with driving. I take I-95 to get home, and traffic is always a nightmare. People drive like absolute maniacs, constantly slamming on their brakes or cutting across three lanes at the last second to make their exit. You really have to pay attention so you don't get rear-ended. And I don't zone out. I don't miss my exit or forget how I got home. I can keep my eyes on the road and track the brake lights in front of me perfectly fine. \n\nSo, yeah, my brain works. The concentration part of it isn't broken at all. I can read a chart, I can drive a car, I can follow a conversation if someone forces me into one. I just... I just don't have the desire to focus on anything unless I absolutely have to for my job or to stay alive. But the actual ability to concentrate? No, I haven't had any trouble with that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Like, my scrubs were hanging so loose I had to pull the drawstring tight enough that it bunched up all the fabric at the waist, and even then, I felt like the pants were going to slide right off my hips when I walked down the hall. I had to keep pulling them up all shift. So, yeah, I'm definitely shrinking down. Just running on empty, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating on things during this period of exhaustion?\n\nPatient: Concentrating? Um... no, not really. I mean, I can focus just fine if I actually have to. It's weird, you'd think with how heavy and exhausted I feel all the time that my brain would be foggy, but it's really not. The focus is still there, it's just the energy to use it that's gone, if that makes sense. \n\nLike, at work, we have this charting software we have to use at the end of every shift. It's called CareTracker, and it's on these really old, slow tablets at the nurse's station. You have to go in and click through all these little drop-down menus for every single resident on your hall—and I usually have like twenty of them. You have to log exactly what they did. Did Mrs. Higgins eat 50% of her dinner or 75%? Did Mr. Abernathy use the toilet on his own, or did he need a two-person assist? Did someone refuse their shower? It's incredibly tedious, and by the end of a twelve-hour shift, I'm literally feeling like my bones are made of lead. But I can sit down at that tablet and just power through it. I don't lose my train of thought, I don't forget whose chart I'm on halfway through, I don't get distracted. I just sit there and focus on the screen and get it done. \n\nAnd it's the same thing with driving. I take I-95 to get home, and traffic is always a nightmare. People drive like absolute maniacs, constantly slamming on their brakes or cutting across three lanes at the last second to make their exit. You really have to pay attention so you don't get rear-ended. And I don't zone out. I don't miss my exit or forget how I got home. I can keep my eyes on the road and track the brake lights in front of me perfectly fine. \n\nSo, yeah, my brain works. The concentration part of it isn't broken at all. I can read a chart, I can drive a car, I can follow a conversation if someone forces me into one. I just... I just don't have the desire to focus on anything unless I absolutely have to for my job or to stay alive. But the actual ability to concentrate? No, I haven't had any trouble with that.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself moving or reacting more slowly than you used to?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 18 / healthcare_support_worker / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Psychomotor_Changes\nmanifestation: \"People at work said I looked slowed down, and I could feel it too because even putting a blood pressure cuff away or answering a question took me way longer than normal.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, actually, I do. It's funny you ask that, because people at work have actually been pointing it out to me. 

Like, my shift supervisor, Brenda, she pulled me aside the other day. Usually, I'm pretty quick on my feet, you know? When you work in a facility, you have to be. Call lights are constantly going off, someone's trying to climb out of bed and they're a fall risk, another person needs to be changed right that second. You're always hustling down the halls. But lately... it's like I'm moving underwater. Brenda actually asked me if I was coming down with the flu or something, because she said I just looked visibly slowed down. Like I was dragging my feet everywhere I went. 

And she wasn't wrong, I could totally feel it, too. It's not just her imagining things. Everything just takes so much more time now. Even the absolute simplest, most mindless stuff. Like, the other day I was doing vitals on my hall. We have these rolling vital sign carts with the automatic blood pressure cuffs and the pulse ox monitors attached to them. I finished taking Mr. Henderson's blood pressure—he's in room 12B—and I just had to put the cuff away. You literally just wrap it up and tuck it into the little wire basket on the front of the cart. That's it. It should take two seconds. But I was just standing there, holding it, and my hands were moving so, so slowly. It felt like it took me five actual minutes just to wrap up this stupid piece of nylon and put it in a basket. It was like my brain, which, like I said, is focusing fine, was sending the signal to my hands to move, but the signal was getting bogged down somewhere along the way. My arms just felt like dead weight. 

And it's the exact same thing with talking or reacting to people. Answering a simple question takes me way longer than normal. One of the nurses will ask me something really basic at the med cart, like, "Hey, did you get fresh ice water for room 7?" And I hear them, the words go into my ears perfectly fine, but then it's like I have to slowly translate it, process it, figure out the answer, and then physically push the words back up my throat. I'll just stand there staring at them blankly for a few seconds before I can even get a simple "yes" or "no" out of my mouth. 

It's embarrassing, honestly. People probably think I'm high on shift or something, but I'm really not. It's just... everything is in slow motion. The whole rest of the building is moving at regular speed, all chaotic and loud, and I'm just stuck in this slow, heavy gear. I can't shift out of it no matter how hard I try to force myself to speed up. So, yeah. Definitely moving a lot slower.

Like, after a long shift at the facility, I'd come home and just zone out for hours. I was really into this one RPG, I had like three hundred hours logged on it, I knew all the lore, I was in these online forums talking about it... and now? Now I look at the controller sitting on my coffee table, and I just feel... nothing. Less than nothing. The thought of even turning the console on feels like a chore. \n\nAnd it's not just the games. It's everything. I used to really like going out with my buddies from high school on the weekends. We'd go grab food, maybe shoot some pool down at this place on 4th Street. But for the last nine months, every time my phone buzzes and it's a text from one of them asking if I want to hang out, I just stare at the screen until it goes dark. I don't even reply half the time. I just can't muster up the energy to pretend I care about what's going on. Food doesn't even taste good anymore. I used to love cooking, making these big meals, and now I'm just eating plain toast or a handful of cereal right out of the box because I can't be bothered. There's just no spark left in anything. It's like the color has been completely drained out of my life, and everything is just this flat, gray, boring mess. I don't look forward to a single thing anymore. I just want to go back to sleep.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about death or wishing you weren't alive during this period of exhaustion?\n\nPatient: No... no, I haven't really had thoughts like that. I mean, I know what you're asking, like if I want to hurt myself or if I'm sitting around wishing I was dead, and... no, it's not like that. It's more just... I'm so incredibly tired, you know? Like I said, just existing right now is so much work. I don't want to die, I just want to stop feeling so heavy all the time. \n\nI guess sometimes I think about how nice it would be to just... sleep. For a really, really long time. Like, if I could just hit pause on the whole world and hibernate for a year, that sounds amazing. But it's not because I want to be dead. It's just because I want a break from this... this slog. Like, I'll be lying in bed, and my alarm goes off at 5:30 in the morning for my shift, and the thought of getting up and putting my scrubs on and driving in the dark to the facility... it's just so overwhelming that I just want to pull the covers over my head and disappear into the mattress. But that's just because I'm exhausted, not because I'm planning anything or wishing I wasn't alive. I just want the energy back. I want to feel like myself again, not this... empty shell of a person who can barely walk across a parking lot. So, no, no thoughts about death, really. Just... wishing I could fast-forward through this part.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge during this period of exhaustion?\n\nPatient: Anxious? Um... no, not really. I mean, not like that nervous, pacing-around kind of anxious. It's funny, because my mom, she gets anxious, right? Like, she's always worrying about the bills, or if the car is making a weird noise, or if I'm eating enough vegetables—which, honestly, I'm not right now, like I said, it's mostly just cereal. But she gets that tight, on-edge feeling where she can't sit still. I see it all the time. But for me, during these last nine months? No. It's the exact opposite of being on edge. \n\nIf anything, I'm... I'm off the edge. I'm at the bottom of the canyon. I don't have the energy to be anxious. Being anxious takes work, you know? Your heart has to beat fast, your brain has to spin with all these \"what ifs.\" My brain isn't spinning. It's barely turning over. Like an engine flooded with water. When I'm at work, at the facility, helping the residents... I used to worry if I was doing everything perfectly, if I was lifting them right, if I was talking to them enough. Now? Now I just go through the motions. I'm too tired to care if I'm doing a perfect job, let alone be anxious about it. I just want to get through the shift so I can go home and lay on the couch. So, no, no real anxiety or feeling on edge. Just... flat. Heavy and flat. I almost wish I was anxious, because at least then I'd be feeling *something* sharp, you know? But it's just this dull, heavy ache of being so, so tired.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling irritable during this period of exhaustion?\n\nPatient: Irritable? Um... no, I wouldn't say irritable. I mean, I know what that looks like, you know? Like when one of the guys at work gets assigned an extra shift and they're just snapping at everyone, slamming the supply closet doors, huffing and puffing down the hallway. I've seen that a million times at the facility. But for me? No. I just... I don't have the energy to be mad or annoyed. It's kind of like what I was saying about the anxiety, it just takes too much effort to be irritable. \n\nLike, the other day, my mom came over. She let herself in because I didn't answer the door—I was just lying on the couch, staring at the ceiling, trying to convince myself to get up and get a glass of water. And she comes in, and she starts talking a mile a minute about her neighbor's dog barking all night, and how she couldn't sleep, and how she's going to write a letter to the homeowners association. Usually, before all this started, that kind of thing would drive me up the wall. I'd be like, \"Mom, please, I just worked a twelve-hour shift, I don't care about Mrs. Gable's poodle.\" I would have definitely snapped at her. But this time? I just laid there. I didn't even turn my head to look at her. I just let the words wash over me like... like static on a radio. I wasn't annoyed, I wasn't mad, I wasn't anything. I just wanted her to finish so it would be quiet again. \n\nAnd it's the same at work. The residents, sometimes they can be a lot, you know? They repeat themselves, or they refuse to take their meds, or they spill their juice on purpose. And some of the other aides, they get so frustrated, they have to step out and take a breather. But me? I just wipe up the juice. I don't feel that spike of annoyance. I just feel... nothing. Just the physical weight of having to bend over and wipe the floor. It's like my fuse isn't even short—it's just completely missing. You can't light it if it's not there. So, no, I'm not irritable. I'm just... I'm just too tired to care enough to be irritated by anything.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you don't enjoy things like playing games or going out with friends anymore, but is there anything at all that you still find interesting or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Interesting or look forward to? Um... no. No, not really. I mean, I've tried to think about that, too. Like, I'll be sitting there, or lying there, usually, and I'll try to run through a list in my head of things that used to make me happy or things I used to want to do. Just to see if anything sparks, you know? Like, I used to really like cars. I'd go to these local car meets on Saturday mornings with my buddy Mark. We'd grab coffee, walk around the parking lot, look at the engines, talk to the owners. It was just a chill way to spend a morning. But now? The thought of going out there, walking around, talking to people... it's just exhausting even thinking about it. \n\nAnd it's not just big things like going out. Even small stuff. Like, I used to look forward to getting off shift and coming home to watch this one sci-fi show I was really into. I'd make a whole thing of it, get some snacks, settle in. But now, I turn the TV on, and it's just... noise. The colors are too bright, the people talking on the screen just seem so far away, and I can't even follow the plot anymore. I just end up turning it off and staring at the blank screen because it's easier. \n\nI don't look forward to meals, either. Like I said, I'm mostly just eating cereal because it's easy, but even then, it's just chewing and swallowing because I know I have to put something in my stomach so I don't pass out at work. It's not like I'm craving a burger or a pizza or anything. It all just tastes like cardboard anyway. \n\nIt's weird, because you'd think there would be *something*, right? Like, one tiny little thing that I'd want to do. But there isn't. It's like my brain just completely forgot how to want things. Or how to enjoy them. I wake up, and I just dread the whole day. Every single part of it. I dread getting out of bed, I dread driving to work, I dread being at work, I dread coming home. There's no bright spot. There's no moment where I think, \"Oh, at least I get to do this later.\" It's just one long, gray, heavy tunnel, and I'm just dragging myself through it. So, no, there's nothing I find interesting anymore. Nothing at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past nine months?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually. I mean, I haven't really been keeping track of the exact numbers or stepping on a scale, because, like I said, I don't really care enough to do that right now. But... yeah. I've definitely lost a lot of weight. \n\nIt kind of ties back into what I was saying about the cereal. I just... I barely want food at all anymore. It's not even that I feel sick to my stomach, it's just that eating feels like this massive chore that I don't want to do. So I just started skipping meals without even really meaning to. Like, I'd completely skip lunch at the facility. Usually, around noon or one, all the other aides pile into the breakroom and it's this whole loud thing with people microwaving leftover pasta and gossiping about the shift supervisors, but I just couldn't deal with the smells or the effort. So I'd just sit in my car or stay on the floor. And then I'd get home after my shift, and I'd be so incredibly exhausted that I'd skip dinner, too. I'd just lie on the couch. \n\nI realized that for, like, weeks on end, I was mostly just drinking coffee or water. Because you don't have to chew water, you know? You just swallow it, and the coffee at least gives you a tiny bit of fake energy to keep you from passing out while you're trying to help a resident to the bathroom. \n\nI didn't even really notice how bad it was getting at first, because scrubs are pretty baggy anyway. But the other day, I was putting on my favorite pair—the navy blue ones, they have these extra cargo pockets on the side that are really great for holding extra gloves and stuff—and I put them on, and they were just hanging off me. Like, my scrubs were hanging so loose I had to pull the drawstring tight enough that it bunched up all the fabric at the waist, and even then, I felt like the pants were going to slide right off my hips when I walked down the hall. I had to keep pulling them up all shift. So, yeah, I'm definitely shrinking down. Just running on empty, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating on things during this period of exhaustion?\n\nPatient: Concentrating? Um... no, not really. I mean, I can focus just fine if I actually have to. It's weird, you'd think with how heavy and exhausted I feel all the time that my brain would be foggy, but it's really not. The focus is still there, it's just the energy to use it that's gone, if that makes sense. \n\nLike, at work, we have this charting software we have to use at the end of every shift. It's called CareTracker, and it's on these really old, slow tablets at the nurse's station. You have to go in and click through all these little drop-down menus for every single resident on your hall—and I usually have like twenty of them. You have to log exactly what they did. Did Mrs. Higgins eat 50% of her dinner or 75%? Did Mr. Abernathy use the toilet on his own, or did he need a two-person assist? Did someone refuse their shower? It's incredibly tedious, and by the end of a twelve-hour shift, I'm literally feeling like my bones are made of lead. But I can sit down at that tablet and just power through it. I don't lose my train of thought, I don't forget whose chart I'm on halfway through, I don't get distracted. I just sit there and focus on the screen and get it done. \n\nAnd it's the same thing with driving. I take I-95 to get home, and traffic is always a nightmare. People drive like absolute maniacs, constantly slamming on their brakes or cutting across three lanes at the last second to make their exit. You really have to pay attention so you don't get rear-ended. And I don't zone out. I don't miss my exit or forget how I got home. I can keep my eyes on the road and track the brake lights in front of me perfectly fine. \n\nSo, yeah, my brain works. The concentration part of it isn't broken at all. I can read a chart, I can drive a car, I can follow a conversation if someone forces me into one. I just... I just don't have the desire to focus on anything unless I absolutely have to for my job or to stay alive. But the actual ability to concentrate? No, I haven't had any trouble with that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself moving or reacting more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually, I do. It's funny you ask that, because people at work have actually been pointing it out to me. \n\nLike, my shift supervisor, Brenda, she pulled me aside the other day. Usually, I'm pretty quick on my feet, you know? When you work in a facility, you have to be. Call lights are constantly going off, someone's trying to climb out of bed and they're a fall risk, another person needs to be changed right that second. You're always hustling down the halls. But lately... it's like I'm moving underwater. Brenda actually asked me if I was coming down with the flu or something, because she said I just looked visibly slowed down. Like I was dragging my feet everywhere I went. \n\nAnd she wasn't wrong, I could totally feel it, too. It's not just her imagining things. Everything just takes so much more time now. Even the absolute simplest, most mindless stuff. Like, the other day I was doing vitals on my hall. We have these rolling vital sign carts with the automatic blood pressure cuffs and the pulse ox monitors attached to them. I finished taking Mr. Henderson's blood pressure—he's in room 12B—and I just had to put the cuff away. You literally just wrap it up and tuck it into the little wire basket on the front of the cart. That's it. It should take two seconds. But I was just standing there, holding it, and my hands were moving so, so slowly. It felt like it took me five actual minutes just to wrap up this stupid piece of nylon and put it in a basket. It was like my brain, which, like I said, is focusing fine, was sending the signal to my hands to move, but the signal was getting bogged down somewhere along the way. My arms just felt like dead weight. \n\nAnd it's the exact same thing with talking or reacting to people. Answering a simple question takes me way longer than normal. One of the nurses will ask me something really basic at the med cart, like, \"Hey, did you get fresh ice water for room 7?\" And I hear them, the words go into my ears perfectly fine, but then it's like I have to slowly translate it, process it, figure out the answer, and then physically push the words back up my throat. I'll just stand there staring at them blankly for a few seconds before I can even get a simple \"yes\" or \"no\" out of my mouth. \n\nIt's embarrassing, honestly. People probably think I'm high on shift or something, but I'm really not. It's just... everything is in slow motion. The whole rest of the building is moving at regular speed, all chaotic and loud, and I'm just stuck in this slow, heavy gear. I can't shift out of it no matter how hard I try to force myself to speed up. So, yeah. Definitely moving a lot slower.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah, I mean, that really goes right back to what I was rambling about earlier when you asked about the weight loss. My appetite is just completely gone. Like I was saying, I barely want food at all anymore. It's just... zero desire. It's not that I feel nauseous or anything, it's just this total lack of interest in eating.

You don't have to chew it or prep it. 

And because I wasn't eating, because that appetite just vanished, that's when I had that whole realization with my favorite navy blue scrubs. Putting them on before my shift and finding out they were hanging so much looser on me, to the point where I was bunching up the fabric with the drawstring just to keep them from sliding off my hips. So, yeah, the appetite change is really the root of all of that weight stuff. I just don't have one anymore.

I'll just stand there staring at them blankly for a few seconds before I can even get a simple \"yes\" or \"no\" out of my mouth. \n\nIt's embarrassing, honestly.
